### Metamorphosis <note>original: "Metamorphosis" – Latin for transformation or change, often referring to alchemical or spiritual transmutation</note>
# of Theophrastus
## Paracelsus / <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a Swiss physician, alchemist, and lay theologian who challenged traditional medical authorities like Galen and Avicenna.</note>

->Also<-

->Three magnificent writings by the same author /<-
->very useful and beneficial to all estates <note>Social ranks or classes</note> / who have been misled by the useless chatter of so-called physicians.<-

->What is treated <note>original: "tractirt"</note> therein, the following leaf after the Preface will indicate.<-

->Published with diligence by Dr. Adam von Bodenstein <note>Bodenstein (1528–1577) was a prominent physician and one of the first major editors of Paracelsus's manuscripts, helping to popularize his work decades after his death.</note> / to commend himself to the highly venerable, his Gracious Prince and Lord, Lord Melchior, Bishop of Basel.<-

->1584.<-

->Endure and Renounce. <note>original: "Ἀνέχου ϗ ἀπέχου" – a famous Greek Stoic motto attributed to the philosopher Epictetus, emphasizing patience and self-control.</note><-

<summary>This is the title page of a 1584 edition of Paracelsus's "Metamorphosis," edited by Adam von Bodenstein and dedicated to the Bishop of Basel. It positions the work as a corrective to the "useless chatter" of traditional physicians of the time.</summary>

Your Princely Grace’s <note>Abbreviated in the text as E. F. G. for 'Eure Fürstliche Gnaden', a standard formal address for a high-ranking noble.</note>

->Humble and devoted servant<-

->*Adam von Bodenstein*<-

->*Doctor.*<-

<summary>This page concludes the dedication of Paracelsus's works, where Adam von Bodenstein formally presents the texts to his noble patron and identifies himself as the philosopher's first public follower and diligent student. It serves as a historical record of the early dissemination of Paracelsian thought in Basel in 1574.</summary>

## Table of Contents for these Books.

These books on natural things / may well be called the *Physics of Paracelsus* <note>original: "physica Paracelsi." In the 16th century, "physics" referred broadly to the study of nature and the physical world.</note> / Because they treat the origin of the body <note>original: "corporis"</note> / life / death / and its faculties / etc.

The remaining works are also natural philosophy <note>original: "physica"</note> / The Manual / concerning the Special *Philosopher’s Stone* <note>original: "Lapide Philosophorum Speciali"</note>.

2 treatises on alchemy <note>original: "tractationes alchimiæ"</note> / concerning the *Stone for transmuting metals* <note>original: "Lapide ad transmutanda metalla"</note>

3. A special treatise concerning the stone <note>original: "de lapide"</note> which Paracelsus sent to his godfather <note>The term "gevatter" implies a close spiritual or family relationship, often a godfather or a close family friend.</note>.

Also three other writings: the first / concerning the *Spirit of Life* <note>original: "Geist deß lebens"</note> and its power.

The second / concerning the power of internal spiritual and physical organs <note>original: "glieder." While literally "limbs," in this medical context it refers to the various parts or organs of the body.</note>.

The third / concerning the power of external organs / and the strengthening of the internal ones.

# Of Theophrastus Paracelsus /
## On Natural Things.
### The First Book.
#### <note>original: "De Generatione"</note> On Generation.

<image-desc>A large ornate decorative initial 'D' filled with dense floral scrollwork and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

The generation <note>The process of birth, creation, or coming-into-being.</note> of all natural things is of two kinds: One, which occurs by nature without any art; and one, which occurs through art, namely, through <term>Alchemy<gloss>The ancient branch of natural philosophy and chemistry aimed at the transmutation of matter.</gloss></term>. Although, to speak of it in general terms, it might be said that all things are born by nature out of the earth with the help of <term>putrefaction<gloss>In alchemy, the process of decay or "blackening" (nigredo) was considered a necessary stage to break down matter so it could be reborn as something higher.</gloss></term>; for putrefaction is the highest <term>degree<gloss>Latin: *gradus*</gloss></term> and the first beginning of generation. This putrefaction takes its start from a moist heat, because constant moist heat brings about putrefaction and transmutes <note>To "transmute" is to fundamentally change a thing's nature, form, or substance.</note> all natural things from their original form and essence, as well as their powers and virtues.

<summary>Paracelsus introduces the two ways things are created—naturally and through alchemy—and emphasizes that decay (putrefaction) caused by moist heat is the essential first step of all new life.</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, alchemy, generation, putrefaction, transmutation, moist heat, nature</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Paracelsus's explanation of putrefaction as the primary mechanism of change, moving from the definition of the process to its practical effects in both biology and alchemy. The catchword "auch" indicates the first word of the following page.</meta>

Then, in the same way that <term>putrefaction<gloss>In alchemy, the process of decay or "blackening" (nigredo) was considered a necessary stage to break down matter so it could be reborn as something higher.</gloss></term> in the stomach turns all food into waste and transmutes <note>To "transmute" is to fundamentally change a thing's nature, form, or substance.</note> it; just so, outside of the stomach, the putrefaction that occurs within a glass vessel transmutes all things from one form into another, from one essence into another, from one color into another, from one scent into another, from one virtue <note>Latin: *virtus*; referring to the inherent medicinal or spiritual power of a substance.</note> into another, from one power into another, from one property into another, and even from one quality into another.

For this is clearly proven and shown by daily experience: many things are good, healthy, and medicinal, but after their putrefaction, they become bad, unhealthy, and a pure poison. Conversely, many things are bad and unhealthy, poisonous and harmful, but after their putrefaction, they become good, lose all their malice, and become a noble medicine. For putrefaction brings forth great things, of which we have a beautiful example

<summary>Paracelsus compares the alchemical process in a laboratory glass to the digestion of the human stomach, explaining how decay can transform poisons into medicines and vice versa.</summary>
<keywords>putrefaction, transmutation, digestion, medicine, poison, alchemy, properties, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Paracelsus's argument regarding the transformative power of putrefaction. also in the holy Gospel, where Christ
says: "Unless the grain of
wheat is cast into the field
and rots, it cannot bear
a hundredfold fruit." <note>This is a reference to John 12:24, a common scriptural justification in alchemy for the necessity of death and decay before spiritual or physical "multiplication" can occur.</note>
In addition, it is to be known that
many things are multiplied in <term>putrefaction<gloss>The process of rotting or fermentation, seen here as the essential first step in any alchemical or biological creation.</gloss></term>

fruit; for putrefaction is a reversal
and the death of all things, and
a destruction of the first essence of
all natural things, from which
comes our <term>rebirth<gloss>German: "widergeburt." In Paracelsian thought, this is the "palingenesis" or the reconstitution of a substance into a higher, purified form.</gloss></term> and new birth
with a thousandfold improvement.

Since putrefaction is now the first
step and beginning of <term>generation<gloss>The act of producing or bringing a new form of life or matter into existence.</gloss></term>,
it is highly necessary that we
recognize putrefaction well, for
there are many kinds of it—indeed,
one brings forth its birth differently
than another, and one much more
quickly than another, etc. Thus it has
now been stated that moisture and
warmth are the first step and
beginning of putrefaction, which
hatches all things <note>The text ends mid-word with "außbrüe," likely intended to be "außbrütet" (hatches or breeds).</note>

<summary>Paracelsus cites the Gospel of John to illustrate that death and decay are prerequisite to growth, defining putrefaction as the essential destruction of an old form that allows for a "thousandfold" improvement in rebirth.</summary>

...whether it is possible by nature and art for a human being to be born outside of a woman’s body and a natural mother. To this I give the answer that it is in no way contrary to the <term>spagyric art<gloss>Original: "Kunst Spagiræ." This refers to Paracelsian alchemy, which focuses on the separation and recombination of natural elements to create medicines.</gloss></term> and nature, but is indeed quite possible. How, however, such a thing may proceed and happen is this: Namely, that the <note>Original: "Dampfe." Literally "vapor" or "steam," here referring to the seminal fluid or the vital spirit contained within it, which was thought to be a specialized kind of foam or vapor.</note> be putrefied in a sealed <term>cucurbit<gloss>Original: "Kükurbiten." A gourd-shaped glass vessel used by alchemists for distillation and fermentation.</gloss></term> with the highest <term>putrefaction<gloss>Original: "putrefaction." In alchemy, this is the process of breaking down a substance through heat and moisture to release its inner life-force.</gloss></term> in a <term>horse’s belly<gloss>Original: "ventre equino." A technical alchemical term for burying a vessel in a pile of decomposing horse manure, which provided a steady, gentle heat for long-term incubation.</gloss></term> for fifty days <note>The text specifies 50 days and 50 weeks, though other versions of this recipe often specify 40 days and 40 weeks to mirror the natural cycle of human gestation.</note>, and that it moves and stirs, which is easily seen. After such a time, it will look somewhat like a little human being, yet transparent and without a solid body. If, after this, it is daily fed and nourished very wisely with the <term>secret essence of human blood<gloss>Original: "arcano sanguinis hominis." An "arcanum" is a hidden or purified spiritual essence of a substance; here, it is blood used as a life-giving "food" for the growing creature.</gloss></term> for fifty weeks, and is kept in the constant, equal warmth of a horse’s belly, it will develop all its limbs just like any other child born of a woman, though much smaller. This we call a <term>homunculus<gloss>Original: "homunculū." Literally "little man"; a miniature, artificially created human being believed to possess great wisdom or magical properties.</gloss></term>.

### Concerning the Generation of Minerals

Regarding the generation of <term>minerals<gloss>Latin: *minerum*.</gloss></term> and half-metals, it is to be understood in no other way than what was initially indicated regarding the metals: that they are likewise born from three <term>principles<gloss>Latin: *principiis*. These are the "Tria Prima" (Three Primes) of alchemy—Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt—which were believed to be the fundamental building blocks of all physical matter.</gloss></term>—namely from <term>Mercury<gloss>Representing the spirit and the liquid/volatile principle.</gloss></term>, <term>Sulfur<gloss>Representing the soul and the oily/combustible principle.</gloss></term>, and <term>Salt<gloss>Representing the body and the solid/stable principle.</gloss></term>. However, [they are] not like the metals, but rather [born] from an imperfect and lesser Mercury, from a lesser and imperfect Sulfur, and from an imperfect and lesser Salt, yet also with their distinct colors.

# Of Theophrastus Paracelsus /
# On Natural Things.

## The Second Book.

### On Things that Grow <note>original: *De Crescentibus*</note>.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>
It is thoroughly evident and common knowledge to everyone that from <term>warmth and moisture<gloss>In the science of the time, these were two of the four primary qualities (along with cold and dryness) believed to govern the growth of all living and material things.</gloss></term> all natural and essential things grow and become more mature. This is sufficiently proven by the rain and by the sun that follows it; for no one can deny that the rain makes the earth fertile, and so one must also acknowledge that the sun makes all fruits <term>ripen<gloss>The original German "zeittich" refers to the process of reaching maturity or a finished state, often used for both crops and alchemical processes.</gloss></term>. Since, according to the <term>ordinance of God<gloss>The natural laws or divine "programming" set by the Creator for the functioning of the universe.</gloss></term>, such a thing is possible for nature and must happen, who would then wish to oppose it?

<summary>Paracelsus begins the second book of his treatise by establishing that warmth and moisture are the fundamental drivers of growth and maturation in nature, using the cycle of rain and sunlight as a primary example of divine order.</summary>
<keywords>growth, warmth, moisture, maturation, natural law, divine order, sun and rain, Theophrastus Paracelsus</keywords>

->wonder.<note>original: "obenthür" (Abenteuer). In this context, it refers to a spectacle, a curiosity, or a marvelous result of an experiment.</note><-

<summary>This page describes an alchemical experiment to rapidly grow a mineral formation inside a glass vessel through repeated cycles of distillation, mimicking and accelerating natural geological processes. <meta>This page marks the beginning of the third book of Paracelsus's treatise. # Theophrastus Paracelsus’/
## On Natural Things.
## The Third Book.
### On Preservation. <note>original: "De Conseruatione."</note>

To maintain a thing and protect it from harm, it is first and foremost necessary to recognize its enemy well, so that one also knows how to protect it from that same enemy, so that it is not injured or destroyed by it in its <term>substance<gloss>The physical matter of an object.</gloss></term>, power, or <term>virtue<note>In early science and alchemy, "virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal or magical power of a substance rather than its moral character.</note></term>—or whatever the damage might be called. For a great deal depends on this: to recognize the enemy in all natural things. For who can guard themselves against harm and accident if they do not know their enemy? No one. Therefore it is necessary to recognize him well, for

<summary>This page introduces the third book of Paracelsus's work, focusing on the preservation of natural objects. Paracelsus argues that the foundational step in protecting any substance's physical integrity and medicinal "virtue" is identifying its natural "enemy" or cause of decay.</summary>

<keywords>preservation, conservation, natural philosophy, Paracelsus, substance, virtue, enemies of nature, decay</keywords>

<summary>Paracelsus argues that understanding destructive "enemies"—such as death, disease, and the devil—is essential for recognizing their opposites: life, health, and God. <meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preserving drinkable liquids mentioned on page 24. Having previously warned against external "impurities," the author now provides specific chemical and herbal remedies—primarily distilled oils—to maintain the quality of wine, beer, mead, vinegar, milk, and cheese.</meta>

Now, the highest **preservative** <note>original: "conseruatiff"</note> and also **preventative** <note>original: "præstruatiff"</note> for wine is **sulfur** and **oil of sulfur**; through these, any wine can be maintained for a very long time, so that it does not become tainted <note>original: "seiger"; referring to the liquid becoming cloudy or separating.</note> nor rise up <note>original: "auffstehet"; likely referring to secondary fermentation or spoiling that causes the liquid to expand or foam.</note>.

The preservative for beer is **oil of cloves** <note>original: "oleū chariophillorū"</note>; a few drops of this should be poured into it—two or three drops for every measure—or an equal amount of the oil of **blessed thistle** <note>original: "benedicten wurzeln"; *Cnicus benedictus*, a plant highly regarded in medieval pharmacy for its curative and preservative powers.</note>, which is much better; this protects the beer from turning sour.

The preservative for **mead** <note>original: "mät"</note> is **oil of sugar** <note>original: "oleum sachari"</note>; this should be used in the same manner as the clove oil or blessed thistle oil mentioned above.

The preservative for vinegar is the oil of **ginger** <note>original: "zinziber"</note>. The preservative for milk is pressed **almond oil**; both of these should be used as previously stated.

The preservative for **cheese** <note>original: "Keß"</note> is the herb **St. John's Wort** <note>original: "hypericō"</note>, which keeps and protects every cheese from worms <note>In a historical context, "worms" usually referred to the larvae of flies or mites that infested stored food.</note>.

<summary>This page details specific preservatives for common household liquids and cheese, recommending distilled oils of sulfur, cloves, ginger, and almonds, as well as the herb St. John's Wort, to prevent souring and infestation.</summary>
<keywords>preservation, wine, beer, mead, vinegar, milk, cheese, oil of sulfur, oil of cloves, blessed thistle, ginger, almond oil, St. John's Wort, food science</keywords>

for when it <note>Referring to St. John's Wort, mentioned on the previous page.</note> is placed with a **cheese** and touches it, no **worm** <note>In this historical context, "worms" usually refers to the larvae of flies or mites that infested stored food.</note> grows within; and if worms were already present and had grown there, they all die and fall out.

**Honey**, however, has no specific **preservative** <note>original: "conservatiff"</note>; it must only be **protected** <note>original: "præseruirt"</note> from its enemy. Now, its greatest enemy is **bread**, for if a little **rye bread** is thrown or falls into it, it turns all the honey into **ants** <note>original: "omeißsen"; this likely refers to the belief that bread caused honey to ferment or attract pests, or it may describe the grainy, "moving" appearance of spoiling honey.</note> and spoils everything.

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the vital spirit. The previous page established that God created no body without a hidden spirit, defining life as a spiritual essence. This page explains the relationship between that spirit and the physical body, specifically regarding life, death, and the spirit's return to the elements.</meta>

For what would the **body** <note>original: *corpus*</note> be worth without the **spirit** <note>original: *spiritum*</note>: nothing. Therefore, the **spirit** possesses the power and virtue, and it lies hidden within [the thing] and not in the **body**. For in the **body** is death; it is also the **subject** <term>subject (original: *subiectum*): the underlying vessel or physical material in which a quality (like life or death) resides.</term> of death, and there is nothing else to be sought or found in it but death, for it may be destroyed in many various ways. 

But the **spirit** [can] never [be destroyed]; it remains always a spirit and living, and is also the **subject** of life. It also keeps its own **body** alive, but in the destruction of the **body** it is separated and divided from it, and leaves the **body** lying dead and returns again to the place from whence it came: namely, into the air and **chaos** <term>chaos: in Paracelsian philosophy, this refers to the "Great Mystery" or the atmospheric void/gas that fills the space between heaven and earth.</term> of the upper and also the lower **firmaments**. 

From this we see that there are many various kinds of **spirits**, just as there are many kinds of **bodies**. For there is a **celestial spirit** <note>original: *spiritus cælestis*</note>, an **infernal spirit** <note>original: *spiritus infernalis*</note>, a **spirit of man** <note>original: *spiritus hominis*</note>, and a **spirit**...

<summary>Paracelsus argues that the body is merely a vessel for death, while the spirit is the true source of life and power. Upon physical death, the indestructible spirit separates from the body and returns to the atmospheric 'chaos' of the firmaments.</summary>
<keywords>dualism, vitalism, spiritus, corpus, chaos, firmament, afterlife of the spirit, Paracelsus</keywords>

...lt <note>original: *salz*; the OCR "lfs" appears to be a transcription of the suffix for salt (salz/saltz)</note>, which then preserves it from rot and stench, and separates and distinguishes itself from it like a water.

Regarding the life of the elements, however, it is to be known that the life of water is its flow; for if it is <term>coagulated|thickened or solidified</term> into an ice and frozen by the <term>firmamental cold|original: *firmamentische kelte*; the celestial cold believed to descend from the heavens</term>, it is dead. All its malice is then taken from it; as long as it is frozen, no one can drown in it anymore.

Likewise, the life of fire is an air, for the air makes the fire burn hotter and stronger. An air also goes forth from every fire, such that it extinguishes a candle light or drives a light little feather upwards into the heights, as one can then plainly see. Therefore, every fire that is living is a **flaming fire** that burns with flames; if it is stopped up and hidden so that no air can escape from it, it must suffocate.

<summary>This page explains the "life" of the elements water and fire, defining water's life as its liquid flow and fire's life as its interaction with air.</summary>
<keywords>elements, water, flow, firmamental cold, ice, fire, air, flaming fire, suffocation, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the four elements (air and earth) before beginning a new book or chapter specifically focused on "Death" as a transformative process in alchemy. This follows the previous discussion of water and fire.</meta>

The air lives for itself and gives life to all other things.

The earth is dead in itself, but its <term>element|In Paracelsian thought, the "element" refers to the spiritual essence or "soul" of the physical matter, rather than the visible soil itself</term> is an invisible, hidden life.

# Of Theophrastus Paracelsus
## Concerning Natural Things.
## The Fifth Book.

->On Death<-
<note>original: *De Morte*</note>

<image-desc>A large, decorative woodcut initial 'D' set within a square frame, featuring intricate foliate and floral scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

**D**eath of all natural things is nothing other than a reversal and change of their <term>powers and virtues|original: *krefften vnd tugenden*; the inherent medicinal, physical, or spiritual properties that define a substance</term>, and a dominion over their goodness and malice, an eradication and suppression of the first nature, and a <term>generation|the alchemical process of birth or the arising of a new state of being</term> of the second and new nature. For you should know this: that very many things are good in life, and great...

...possess <term>power and virtue|original: *krafft vnd tugend*; the inherent active properties and medicinal efficacy of a substance</term>; but when they die, are dead, or are killed, they have little or nothing more of their power and virtue, instead appearing quite dull and powerless. Conversely, many things are evil in life, but in death, or after they have been killed, they appear in manifold power and virtue and are quite distinct. ### Alchemist's preparation,
wherein many such highly hidden things are discovered. For look at <term>living mercury|original: *mercurium viuum*; liquid mercury or quicksilver</term>, <term>crude and living sulfur|original: *sulphur viuū vnd crudum*</term>, and <term>crude antimony|original: *antimonium crudum*</term> as they come out of the mountain—that is, while they are in their "living" state. How small is their <term>power and virtue|original: *krafft vnd tugend*; the inherent active properties and medicinal efficacy of a substance</term>, and how weak and sluggish is their effect! Furthermore, they cause much more harm than good; they are more of a poison than a medicine.
But when these are completely destroyed from their first essence by the alchemist's clever handiwork and skill, "killed," and wisely prepared—such as when the mercury is <term>coagulated|original: *coaguliert*; turned from a liquid to a solid</term>, <term>precipitated|original: *præcipitirt*; separated out of a solution</term>, <term>sublimated|original: *sublimirt*; purified by heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then a solid again</term>, <term>resolved|original: *resoluiert*; dissolved or reduced to a simpler form</term>, and brought into an <term>oil|original: *oleum*; in alchemy, a concentrated, potent liquid state regarded as the "soul" of the mineral</term>.
When the sulfur is sublimated, <term>calcined|original: *calciniert*; reduced to a powder or "calx" through intense dry heat</term>, <term>reverberated|original: *reuerberiert*; heated in a furnace where the flames are reflected back onto the substance</term>, and also brought into an oil.
Likewise, when the antimony is sublimated, calcined, reverberated, and also brought into an oil—then you shall see what great usefulness, what great power

<summary>Paracelsus explains that raw minerals like mercury, sulfur, and antimony are toxic and weak in their natural state, but through alchemical "death" and laboratory processing, they are transformed into powerful, useful medicines.</summary>

...ing, <term>cementing|original: cemētiren; a process of refining metal by heating it in a furnace with powdered chemical mixtures</term>, and <term>subliming|original: sublimierē; the process of turning a solid into vapor and then back into solid form to purify it</term>.

The **calcination** of metals, however, is not of one kind alone; for one happens through salt, one through <term>sulfur|original: sulphur</term>, one through <term>strong water|original: aquam fortem; nitric acid, used to dissolve metals</term>, one through common mercury, and one through <term>living mercury|original: mercurium viuum; a term often used for quicksilver in its natural or alchemically "vital" state</term>.

The calcination through salt is done by <term>laminating|original: laminiert; beating or rolling metal into thin plates</term> the metal thin, <term>stratifying|original: stratificier; arranging in alternating layers</term> it with salt, and cementing it.

But the calcination through sulfur is thus: that one laminates the metal and also stratifies it with sulfur, and <term>reverberates|original: reuerberier; heating the material in a furnace where the flame is deflected or "reverberated" back onto the substance</term> it.

The calcination that happens through the <term>strong water|original: aqua fort</term> is when one <term>granulates|original: granulier; turning metal into small grains, usually by pouring the molten metal into water</term> the metal and <term>dissolves|original: resoluier</term> it in the strong water, and <term>precipitates|original: niderschlag; causing the dissolved metal to settle out of the liquid as a solid powder</term> it therein.

The calcination through common mercury is thus: that one laminates the metal and puts the mercury into an earthen vessel that is wide at the bottom and narrow at the top, and sets it upon or in a good <term>charcoal fire|original: kolfewr</term>, and blows upon it a little <note>likely using bellows to increase the heat</note> until the mercury...

**Dead iron** is such a <term>vitriol|original: vitriol; a sulfate salt, often green in the case of iron</term>; likewise, <term>burnt copper|original: aes ustum</term> is a killed copper. Every <term>mercury sublimate|original: mercurius sublimatus</term>, <term>precipitate|original: præcipitatus</term>, <term>calcined mercury|original: calcinatus</term>, cinnabar, etc., is a killed quicksilver. Every <term>azure|original: lasur; often referring to blue copper or silver carbonates</term> is a killed silver.

You then have a **dead earth** <note>A base material stripped of its active properties.</note>, in which there is no longer anything of power.

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the mortification of various substances. The previous page concluded the discussion on metallic ores whose "life" or spirit is released through sublimation with salt or vitriol.</meta>

The mortification of **sweet things** <note>original: *dulcedinischen dingen*, from the Latin *dulcedo* (sweetness).</note> is that they are **sublimated** and **distilled** with **corrosive** substances.

<meta>This page continues the list of how various substances are "mortified" to release their inner essence. The previous page discussed the mortification of minerals, aromatics, resins, and herbs.</meta>

The *mortification* <term>mortification</term> <note>In alchemy, to "mortify" a substance means to "kill" its outward form—often through fire, decay, or chemical change—to release the spiritual essence hidden within.</note> of **wood** is that it be burned to coal or ashes.

The *mortification* of **bones** is *calcination* <term>calcination</term> <note>original: *calcinatio*. The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to a powder or ash.</note> or ashing.

The *mortification* of **flesh** and **blood** is the removal of its **spirit of salt** <note>original: *spiritus salis*. In this context, it refers to the essential preservative mineral quality that prevents rot; its removal allows the body to break down.</note>.

The *mortification* of **water** happens through **fire**, for the fire or the heat of the fire dries out and consumes all water.

Likewise, the *mortification* of **fire** happens through **water**, for the water extinguishes the fire and takes from it its power and effect, etc.

Thus you have received a sufficient account in few words of how **death** is hidden in all natural things, and how they should be **mortified** and brought into another nature and essence <note>original: *wesen*.</note>, and what manner of power and virtues flow out from them. # [Of] Theophrastus Paracelsus
### On Natural Things.
## The Fifth Book.

->On Resuscitation.<- <note>original Latin: *De resuscitatione*. In alchemy, this refers to the process of restoring a substance to its life or original power after it has been chemically altered or "killed" through mortification.</note>

The *resuscitation* <term>resuscitation</term> and restoration of all natural things is no small thing in nature, but rather a high and great mystery—and more divine and angelic <note>Paracelsus uses "angelic" to describe knowledge or processes that belong to a higher, celestial order beyond common human understanding.</note> than human and natural. Furthermore, it should be understood with great discernment, and not interpreted by anyone differently than I intend, and as nature herself daily and visibly proves, and as experience <note>original: *erfahrung*. For Paracelsus, direct observation and trial were more important than ancient textbooks.</note> itself demonstrates.

<summary>This page introduces the fifth book of Paracelsus's treatise, moving from the concept of "mortification" (chemical destruction) to "resuscitation" (restoration). He describes this process as a divine mystery that transcends ordinary human nature and must be proven through direct experience.</summary>

<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, resuscitation, natural things, divine mystery, experience, restoration, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Fifth Book of Paracelsus’s work. Having established that "resuscitation" is a divine-like mystery, he now addresses his critics and begins to define the technical boundaries of how a substance can be "brought back to life" after being chemically altered.</meta>

So that I am not accused of untruths and slandered by the so-called physicians—my adversaries—(who, besides all that, interpret everything I do in the worst possible way), claiming that I wish to usurp God's power and attribute to nature that which has never been possible for her:

Therefore, it is very and highly necessary to pay attention here, since death is divided into two parts: namely, into *dying* and *killing* <note>original German: *sterben* and *tödten*. In Paracelsus's theory, "dying" refers to a natural end, while "killing" refers to an artificial or premature change in state, such as a chemical reaction.</note>. Only one of these may be resuscitated <term>resuscitated</term> and restored again, while the other cannot. For this reason, you should give no credit to the sophists <note>Paracelsus used "sophists" as a derogatory term for the traditional academic physicians of his time, whom he viewed as prioritizing logic and old textbooks over practical results.</note> who say that whatever has once died or been killed can never be resuscitated, and who think nothing at all of resuscitation and restoration—which is no small error on their part. It is certainly true that whatever dies its natural death, and whatever nature kills according to predestination <term>predestination</term> <note>In this context, "predestination" refers to the predetermined natural lifespan or the inherent "internal clock" of a substance or living thing.</note>, over that, man has

<meta>catchword: no [kein]</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus defends his alchemical theories against rival physicians, arguing that there is a crucial distinction between natural death and a "killed" state. He asserts that while natural death is irreversible, substances that have been "killed" through chemical processes can indeed be resuscitated.</summary>

<keywords>resuscitation, restoration, death vs killing, sophists, predestination, alchemical theory, Paracelsus, nature's limits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s argument regarding the limits of human versus divine power in the context of alchemy and "resuscitation." He establishes that while humans can manipulate what they have made, only God or a person with supernatural faith can restore what nature has naturally "consumed."</meta>

no power to resuscitate <term>resuscitate</term> <note>In Paracelsus’s alchemical theory, "resuscitation" refers to the act of bringing a substance back to its original state or "life" after it has been chemically altered or "killed."</note> the same; only God has this, or it must happen by Divine command. Likewise, what nature consumes, man also cannot restore. However, that which man breaks apart, he can also make again; and that which he has made, he can break apart again. Beyond this, man has no power by his own nature. Whoever would venture further, he desires to grasp at God’s own power, and would exert himself in vain, becoming a laughingstock in the process. Unless, however, God were with him, or he possessed such faith that he could move mountains—for such a man, this would indeed be possible to do, and much more besides. For the Scripture says, and Christ himself has spoken: "If you have faith as a grain of mustard seed <note>original German: *sänffkörnlin*. A reference to the biblical parable in Matthew 17:20, where Jesus tells his disciples that faith can move mountains.</note>, and say to this mountain, 'Move from here to there,' it will [do it]"

<meta>catchword: do [thun]</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus defines the boundaries of alchemical practice, asserting that while humans can reverse their own chemical "breaking" of substances, only God or divine faith can restore what nature has naturally ended.</summary>

do [it], and set itself [elsewhere], and everything will be possible for you and nothing will be impossible, etc. <note>original: "zc." abbreviation for "et cetera."</note> Now, however, so that we may return to our undertaking—namely, what finally the difference is between "dying" and "being killed," and which of these two may be resuscitated—it is to be understood in this way:

## The Sixth Grade.

Thus **coagulation** <note>The process by which a liquid or fluid substance is transformed into a solid or semi-solid state.</note> is also the sixth grade. Now, there are two types of coagulation: one of **cold**, and one of **heat**. That is, one of the **air**, and one of the **fire**. And each of these is further divided into two parts and doubled; and thus there are four: two of cold and two of heat. But the coagulations of the heat of fire are **fixed** <note>original: *fix*. Chemically stable and resistant to further change by heat.</note>; the other coagulations of cold are **unfixed** <note>original: *vnfix*. Volatile or easily reverted to a liquid state.</note>; and the one coagulation of the

...cold from the common air alone, or simply outside of the fire. This is from the **artificially graded fire** <note>Fire whose temperature is carefully controlled by degrees for specific chemical effects.</note> of the **alchemist** and is **fixed** and permanent; for whatever such fire coagulates, it remains so. 

The other coagulation is from the **volcanic** <note>original: *Ethnischen*. Derived from Mount Etna, referring to the natural subterranean heat of the earth.</note> and mineral fire in the mountains, which fire the **Archaeus of the earth** <term>Archæus terræ: the "inner artisan" or vital spirit within the earth that directs the formation of minerals and metals</term> grades in a manner not very different from the alchemist. And what it coagulates through such fire is also **fixed** and permanent. You can see this in the **metals** and **minerals**, which are initially all a **mucilaginous** <note>original: *mucilaginische*. A thick, slimy, or jelly-like state of matter.</note> material, and are thus coagulated by the volcanic fire in the mountains through the Archaeus of the earth and the laborer of nature into metals, stones, iron, salts, and other **bodies** <note>original: *corporibus*. Solid substances or chemical compounds.</note>.

# The Seventh Degree.

Tincturing <note>original: *Tingieren*. The alchemical process of coloring or dyeing a substance to fundamentally change its internal nature.</note>, however, is the seventh and
final **degree** <note>original: *gradus*. An essential stage or step in the alchemical sequence.</note> which concludes the
entire work of our secret for **transmu-
tation** <note>The transformation of one substance into another, most famously the turning of base metals into gold.</note> and makes all imper-
fect things perfect, and **transmutates**
them into the noblest essence, into
its highest health and purity,
and changes it into another color.
Now, a **tincture** <note>original: *Tinctura*. A concentrated alchemical agent capable of imbuing other materials with its specific qualities and permanence.</note> is a very noble
matter with which one tinctures the me-
tallic and human **bodies** <note>original: *corpora*. Referring to physical matter or living organisms.</note>,
and improves them into a much nobler es-
sence, into their highest health and pu-
rity; it colors all things according to its
own color and nature. However, there
are many and various types of tinctures,
and not only tinctures of metallic
and human **bodies** <note>original: *corporum*. The plural form of physical substances.</note>; for
anything that passes through, pene-
trates, or dyes another matter
into another color, essence, or form—
so that it is no longer like its
original state—may be called a tincture.

<meta>This page transitions from the general natural transmutations discussed on the previous page—such as wood turning to stone or flax to silk—to the specific alchemical processes used to transform and refine metals, specifically focusing on the method of "cementation."</meta>

# Theophrastus Paracelsi /
# On the Transformation of Metals.
## The Ninth Book.

### On Cementations.
<note>original: *De cimentis*</note>
#### Preface.

In the same manner as we have recounted concerning other <term>transmutations</term> <note>original: *transmutationibus*; the changing of one substance into another, particularly base metals into gold</note>, we also wish to make our <term>operations</term> <note>original: *operationes*; specific laboratory procedures or chemical works</note> understood by means of <term>cementations</term> <note>original: *cimenten*; a dry alchemical process where a metal is packed in a reactive powder and heated to purify it or change its color</note>. In these, six kinds of cementation are to be considered, which are explained separately according to their recipes and procedures. Yet this is to be observed in all of them: that all cementations ought to result in a <term>fixation</term> <note>original: *fixation*; the process of making a substance stable and resistant to the effects of fire</note> and a <term>coloration</term> <note>original: *coloration*; the imparting of a permanent golden hue to a metal</note>. And these two qualities should not depart from one another, but rather remain unbreakable and persist through the <term>ash-test</term> <note>original: *cinerico*; referring to cupellation, a refining process where metal is heated in a vessel made of bone-ash</note>, through coloring, and through <term>quartation</term> <note>original: *quartierung*; a method of separating gold from silver by adding silver until the gold is only one-quarter of the alloy, then dissolving the silver with acid</note>, just as the characteristics of true gold are found within them.

<summary>Paracelsus introduces the ninth book on metallic transformation, focusing on "cementation" as a method to achieve the permanent color and stability (fixation) required for a metal to pass the tests of true gold.</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus Paracelsus, metallurgy, cementation, transmutation, fixation, coloration, cupellation, quartation, alchemy</keywords>

Reduce the **slag** <note>original: *schlacken*; the stony waste matter separated from metals</note> to see what **silver** <note>original: *luna*; literally "the moon," the alchemical name for silver</note> remains within it.

**Experience** <term>practical observation</term> has shown us that a short fire during the **cementation** <term>the process of heating metal in a dry chemical powder to refine it</term> will yield nothing useful. ## The Second Cement 
### For the Sun <term>original: *ad Solem*; for gold</term>.

Such metal has not been made sufficiently **fixed** <term>stable and non-volatile</term> in its place due to a failure in its **operation** <term>the specific chemical procedure</term>.

# The fifth cement / for
## volatile bodies.

or **gold** <note>original: *solis*; the Sun</note>. And by this it is also to
be understood that the **corporal mercuries** <note>original: *mercurij corporales*; the "essences" extracted from the metals themselves</note>
of the metals have a distinction
from **common mercury** in their **tincture** <term>tincture: in alchemy, a substance or "dye" capable of transmuting and coloring base metals into precious ones</term>:
For they provide a more significant and greater part of the
tinctures of the tested **body** <note>original: *corporis*; refers to the metal being worked upon</note>
than the common mercuries provide.
It is also to be understood that the
**corporal and common mercuries**
should first be **coagulated** <note>original: *coaguliert*; turned from a liquid or vaporous state into a solid</note>,
so that they may withstand

part with part <note>original: *pars cum parte*; a specific alchemical process for refining precious metals by mixing them in equal proportions</note>. In this, you should understand that "part with part" receives significantly more tincture <note>the coloring or purifying essence of the alchemical medicine</note> and does so faster than the other operations <note>technical processes</note> of the artists <note>alchemists</note>. For this is the reason: that the gold <note>original: *sol*; the Sun, representing gold</note> is fixed <note>made resistant to evaporation by fire</note> and also graded <note>improved in color and quality</note> along with the cements. 

And it is to be understood that both have been previously cemented and purged <note>purified of dross</note> in the most subtle and purest manner. Afterward, they are melted together in equal parts, laminated <note>hammered into thin sheets</note>, and arranged in equal parts layer upon layer <note>original: *stratum super stratum*</note> one upon the other, and sealed very tightly in a crucible <note>original: *tigel*</note>. 

And such a <term>cement <gloss>a reactive powder used in a dry refining process to purify metals at high heat</gloss></term> powder is made thus:

**Take: Cinnabar,**
**Flowers of copper <note>original: *floris æris*; verdigris or copper oxide</note>,**
**Hematite <note>original: *hæmatitis*; a reddish iron ore</note>—of each 1 lot <note>roughly 15 grams</note>,**
**Sal ammoniac <note>original: *salis armoniaci*; ammonium chloride</note>,**
**Calamine <note>original: *calaminaris*; zinc ore</note>,**
**Sulfur—of each 1 lot,**
**Saffron of Mars <note>original: *croci martis*; iron oxide or rust</note>—2 lots,**
**Common salt,**
**Vitriol <note>original: *vitrioli*; sulfate minerals</note>,**
**Alum—of each 1 lot.**

Mix these together, well-pounded. # Theophrastus Paracelsus /
## Of the transformation of metals.
### The Tenth Book.

->Concerning gradations.<-

So we wish now to begin speaking of the <term>gradations <gloss>alchemical processes used to improve the quality, color, or "grade" of a metal</gloss></term> <note>original: *gradatzen*</note> / which are of such a nature / that they may <term>graduate</term>, convert, and <term>transmute <gloss>to change one substance into another, specifically base metals into gold</gloss></term> the <term>Luna <gloss>the alchemical name for silver</gloss></term> <note>original: *lunam*</note> / which is dissolved, extinguished, or <term>digested <gloss>heated steadily over a long period</gloss></term> therein / into <term>Sol <gloss>the alchemical name for gold</gloss></term> <note>original: *solem*</note> / Although there are some who work upon <term>Luna <gloss>silver</gloss></term> [derived] from the lesser metals / and with a distinction of the <term>conjunction <gloss>the union or joining of different substances</gloss></term> of the metals together / so that at last through the transmutation of the metals in the gradation / the lesser metals are brought into the greater / and remain within them.

<summary>Paracelsus introduces Book 10, focusing on the process of "gradation," which he defines as the method for transforming silver or base metals into gold.</summary>

Thus, fourteen kinds of <term>gradations</term> <note>original: *gradationes*</note> are described, under which all [other] gradations are understood; and although there are many more, we leave them out and assemble those that have been tested, which are well worth describing for the sake of gradation. And it is to be understood that some are of the nature of <term>strong waters <gloss>corrosive mineral acids, typically nitric acid</gloss></term> <note>original: *aqua fortische*</note> / some are oils / and some are also <term>liquors <gloss>liquid substances or solutions, often referring to alchemical spirits</gloss></term>. What equipment and form, however, may be taken and used for them, these will be prescribed and explained in order for each one; and so we set the strong waters first, then the oils, and at last

# The second gradation by
## strong waters.

Thus, concerning the second <term>gradation <gloss>a process used to improve the purity or color of a metal, often to transform it toward gold</gloss></term>, another process and formula with greater profit and subtlety is to be noted, and it is as follows. Take 1 pound of <term>Saltpeter <gloss>potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in making acids</gloss></term> <note>original: *Salis petræ*</note> and 1 pound of <term>Cinnabar <gloss>mercury sulfide</gloss></term> <note>original: *cinnabrij*</note> / crush them together and distill a water from them / keep that. Also do the same in the same weight with <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: *Antimonio*</note> / and with <term>Arsenic</term> <note>original: *arsenico*</note> / Pour these three waters together / add to them 1 pound each of <term>Alum <gloss>a mineral salt</gloss></term> <note>original: *aluminis*</note> / <term>Saltpeter</term> / and <term>Vitriol <gloss>metallic sulfates</gloss></term> <note>original: *vitrioli*</note> / Distill them together / like a <term>strong water <gloss>a corrosive mineral acid, typically nitric acid</gloss></term> <note>original: *aqua fort*</note> / afterward put them into a <term>flask <gloss>a round glass vessel used in distillation</gloss></term> <note>original: *kolbē glaß*</note> / with the <term>dead head <gloss>the solid residue or "caput mortuum" left behind after distillation</gloss></term> <note>original: *todtē kopff*</note> well-ground / and distill it once more until the very last spirits / afterward purify this like a strong water. All the metals / that are dissolved therein / and remain in <term>digestion <gloss>the process of applying constant, gentle heat to a substance</gloss></term> toward <term>perfection <gloss>the state of being fully transformed into gold or silver</gloss></term> <note>original: *perfectionem*</note> / more than can be believed / it accomplishes its effect in a strange and wonderful manner:

<summary>This page describes the second method of "gradation," a process for refining or transforming metals using a complex "strong water" distilled from saltpeter, cinnabar, antimony, and arsenic. The resulting acid is intended to dissolve metals and bring them to "perfection" through digestion.</summary>
<keywords>gradation, strong water, distillation, saltpeter, cinnabar, antimony, arsenic, vitriol, alchemy, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the second gradation's effects and potency, specifically its power to color metals, before transitioning into the recipe for the third and final method of refining metals using corrosive mineral acids.</meta>

For in it is the true power of **transmutation over all metals** <note>original: *transmutationis super omnia metalla*</note> / which possess a physical **malleability** <note>original: *malleation* — the property of a metal that allows it to be hammered into sheets</note> within themselves: For the first three distillations put and bring the colors so powerfully into the **strong water** <note>original: *aqua fort* — a term for a corrosive mineral acid, usually nitric acid</note>, that its equal among **strong waters** <note>original: *aquas fortes*</note> can hardly be found.

### The third gradation by
### strong waters.

This third <term>gradation <gloss>a process used to improve the purity or color of a metal, often to transform it toward gold</gloss></term>, which is then also in the form of waters, and is the last, shall also be specially understood, and it is as follows.
Take <note>original: *Rec.*</note> <term>Cinnabar <gloss>mercury sulfide, a bright red mineral</gloss></term> <note>original: *Cinnabrij*</note> /
<term>Arsenic</term> /

Then set it to <term>coagulate <note>to thicken or solidify a liquid into a solid mass.</note></term> and melt it down into <term>lead <note>original: *Bley*; referring to the process of cupellation where impurities are absorbed by lead.</note></term> and <term>refine it <note>original: *fulminiers*; to reach the final point of purity where the metal "flashes" on the cupel.</note></term>. And it is to be understood that common <term>mercury <note>original: *mercurius vulgi*</note></term> and the mercury of metals coagulates and transmutes therein, according to the nature of transmutation.

## The fourteenth gradation, which
### is the fourth watery liquid.

Take mercury <term>sublimate <note>original: *sublimati*; a substance purified by heating it into vapor and then collecting the solid deposit.</note></term> with <term>sal ammoniac <note>original: *sale amoniaco*; ammonium chloride, used to assist in the sublimation of metals.</note></term>, [processed] twenty times;
Flowers of copper <note>original: *Flores veneris*; "Flowers of Venus."</note>;
Flowers of <term>iron <note>original: *croci martis*; literally "Saffron of Mars," a red iron oxide.</note></term>;
Flowers of sulfur;
Flowers of <term>antimony <note>original: *antimonij*; a brittle, silvery-white metalloid.</note></term>;
Two <term>lots <note>original: *lot*; a unit of weight roughly equal to 15-17 grams.</note></term> of each.

The <term>water <note>In alchemy, "water" often refers to a corrosive acid or a solvent rather than plain water.</note></term> however requires no further work at all, since the metals lie and <term>digest <note>original: *digerieren*; to soften or transform a substance through gentle, consistent heat.</note></term> within it until its brief and appointed time. # Manual / of Theophrastus Paracelsus / on the Philosopher’s Stone.

## Preface of Aureolus Paracelsus / to the Reader.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' featuring scrolling foliate patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

Since God fundamentally began to work the spirit of medicine <note>original: *artzney*</note> through Machaon, Podalyrius, Apollo, Hippocrates, etc. <note>These are figures from Greek mythology and history: Machaon and Podalyrius were sons of the healer-god Asclepius; Apollo was the god of healing; Hippocrates is the famous physician of antiquity.</note>—so that true medicine might appear and break forth into the light as if through a cloud (in which it could not be very clearly recognized) and be partially revealed to mankind—so He has also prevented the spirit of darkness from completely suppressing and extinguishing the <term>light of nature <note>original: *liecht der natur*; for Paracelsus, this is the divine wisdom and intuition granted to humans to understand the secrets of the physical world and healing.</note></term>.

<sig>m</sig>

<summary>This preface introduces a manual on the Philosopher's Stone, where Paracelsus frames the history of medicine as a divine gift passed through ancient healers. # Concerning the manifold fire
## through which many and various
## metals are born.

I have written concerning the

# Theophrastus Paracelsus
### Concerning Tinctures.
## The Second Book.

In the first book, I wrote of the spirits or **tinctures**, and what properties or natures they contain within themselves, and what metal belongs to each. In this book, I will write of the medium—that is, the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: "mercurio philosophorū." This is not common quicksilver, but a purified, spiritual substance that alchemists believed was the "First Matter" of all metals.</note> or the **Mercury of the Wise**—through which the **tinctures** or the **leaven** <term>leaven: Much like yeast in bread, alchemical leaven is a substance added to "ferment" a metal, allowing it to grow and transform into a higher state.</term> of the metals is made. For this reason, I also wish to divide this little book into seven parts.

<meta>Catchword: In</meta>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Tinctures, Mercury of the Philosophers, Alchemy, Leaven, Metallic Spirits</keywords>

In the first chapter, I wish to write of that from which the **tinctures** are made. In the second, how much of each one should take. In the third, how the glasses should be, so that they are neither too large nor too small. In the fourth, how one should maintain the fire. In the fifth, what signs appear when the **Mercury of the Wise** <note>original: "mercurius der weiſen"</note> acts upon the common [metals]. In the sixth, by what one shall recognize when the **tinctures** are complete. In the seventh, how one should thereafter multiply them, so that one part may **tinge** <term>tinge: From the Latin 'tingere,' meaning to dye or color; in alchemy, this refers to the transformative power of a tincture to change the nature of a base metal into gold or silver.</term> a hundred parts.

# Of the properties of fire.
<sig>q</sig>

After you have placed the **matter** <note>original: "materiam"</note> into the correct glasses, then place it in natural warmth, in such a way that the external warmth does not exceed the internal warmth. For if the heat were too great, no **union** <note>original: "vereinigung"; referring to the alchemical conjunction or "marriage" of substances.</note> could occur, because great heat scatters and burns the matter so that nothing good can come of it or grow from it anymore.

And for this reason, if the **airy part** <note>The author uses a cosmological metaphor: just as the atmosphere protects the Earth from the direct fire of the sun, the alchemist must use a medium to buffer the heat.</note> between heaven and earth were not properly ordered, the sun would burn the stems of all creatures and creations so that nothing could grow there. Therefore, attend to the airy, moist part; place it between the fire and the **matter** <note>original: "materien"</note>. In this way, the heat cannot so easily harm the work, nor scatter and burn it. 

Conversely, if the **living fire** <term>Living fire: A steady, vital heat—often compared to the warmth of a brooding hen—required to animate the alchemical process without destroying the delicate "spirits" within.</term> were to be too small, then the **spirit** <note>original: "geist"</note> of the matter would smoke, and

<summary>This page discusses the crucial role of heat regulation in alchemy, warning that excessive heat prevents the union of substances by "burning" the matter, much like the sun would destroy life without the atmosphere.</summary>
<keywords>fire, warmth, matter, union, spirit, alchemy, heat regulation, living fire</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 61, concluding the discussion on the delicate balance of heat before transitioning into the signs that accompany the alchemical union.</meta>

[it] would not act with its **moisture** <note>original: "humido (.i. Nesse)"</note> and [would] dry them out and **fix** <term>Fixation: The alchemical process of "fixing" a substance, transforming it from a volatile or liquid state into a solid form that can endure fire without evaporating.</term> them. For the spirits of the metals are dead and lie at rest; they cannot work unless they are made alive by living fire. The same also happens in the **macrocosm** <note>original: "grossen welt," referring to the universe or the natural world at large.</note>: the seed in the earth is dead and cannot grow by itself; it must be made alive by the sun. 

The same thing happens here in our work; and for this reason, the practitioner should take care that he governs the fire to be neither too small nor too great. Otherwise, it will come to no proper conclusion, even if he has used all other things correctly.

# The Fifth Chapter.
## Concerning the signs that occur

# The Third Book.

I have written in the second book by what means one should make the **tinctures** <term>Tinctures: Concentrated alchemical substances used to transform metals.</term> or **ferments** <term>Ferments: Agents added to a substance to "leaven" or trigger a specific chemical transformation, similar to yeast in bread.</term>; now I will write how one should make the **tincture of the sun** <note>original: "tincturam solem," referring to the Golden Tincture.</note> and the **moon** <note>original: "lunam," referring to the Silver Tincture.</note>, and I will divide this little book into ten parts.

In the first: how one should make the sun, with the planets and the firmament, which is the furnace with the fire.

## The First Chapter.

Thus speaks **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy and the "father of all the wise."</note>, a father of all the wise of this **Art** <term>Art: A standard historical term for Alchemy.</term>: make, create a new world; for just as Christ has created heaven and earth, so also must the furnace with its sun be created, and be governed in like manner; therefore I will write how you should make the "world" for this art. First, make a square furnace to the height of six spans <note>A "span" (spanne) is an old unit of measurement based on the distance between the tip of the thumb and the tip of the little finger, roughly 9 inches.</note>.

# The Sixth Chapter.

This Queen cures all diseases.

through it. Amen.

## The First Chapter.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' decorated with intricate scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc> I, Philippus Theophrastus Bombast <note>The birth name of the physician and alchemist better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541). He was famous for his radical rejection of traditional medicine in favor of chemical remedies.</note>, say that following the revelation of Divine grace concerning the *Tincture of the Physicists* <note>original: "tinctur phisicorum" — a legendary alchemical substance believed to possess extraordinary healing powers and the ability to transmute base metals into gold.</note>, many different paths have been sought, and yet ultimately all have arrived at one desired end. For this reason, Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary figure representing the fusion of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian Thoth; he was considered the founder of alchemy and "Hermetic" philosophy.</note> the Egyptian went to work according to his own mind. Horus the Greek <note>An ancient figure often cited in alchemical literature, sometimes conflated with the Egyptian deity.</note> followed a similar process. Hali <note>Likely referring to Khalid ibn Yazid, an early Arabic prince associated with the beginnings of alchemy.</note>, an Arabic master, remained within his own order. Albertus the German <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and saint who was widely credited with alchemical discoveries in the centuries following his death.</note> also followed the long path. Thus, each of these men followed his own head, and yet they all finally arrived at one destination—namely, long life.

<summary>Paracelsus opens the first chapter of his work by claiming divine inspiration for his medical-alchemical secrets and citing famous historical alchemists from Egypt, Greece, Arabia, and Germany as his predecessors in the search for longevity.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Tincture of the Physicists, Hermes Trismegistus, Horus, Hali, Albertus Magnus, alchemy, longevity, history of medicine</keywords>

In the same way, the **light of nature** <term>light of nature</term> <note>original: "liecht der natur" — in Paracelsian philosophy, this is the innate wisdom placed by God within the natural world, which a physician or alchemist must learn to read through observation.</note> was created so that through it, one might see the test or proof of every thing and walk in that very light <note>original: "lumine" — light.</note>. Therefore, on the basis of my foundation, many a peasant has certainly become a nobleman. In contrast, many a nobleman, through that supposed art <note>Referring to the misguided or "false" alchemy of those who only read books without laboratory experience.</note>, has become a peasant again; he carried mountains of gold in his head before he ever put his hand into the coals.

Therefore, before I come to the process of the

## The Sixth Chapter

If the **tincture of the physicists** <term>Tincture of the Physicists (Latin: tinctura physicorum): A potent alchemical substance, often equated with the Philosophers' Stone, believed to have the power to "cure" base metals and human illness.</term> is to be brought to **transmutation** <term>Transmutation: The alchemical process of changing one element or metal into another, specifically "base" metals like lead into "noble" ones like gold.</term>, then first, one part of it must be cast upon a thousand parts of molten **gold** <note>original: "solis" — Literally "of the sun," the traditional alchemical name for gold.</note>. Then the medicine is ready to change the leprous **moisture of the metals** <note>original: "humorem metallorum" — Alchemists believed that base metals were "sick" or "leprous" versions of gold, suffering from an imbalance of internal fluids or "humors" that the tincture could heal.</note>. 

Let that be a special and great wonder in the light of nature: that through the work of the **spagyrist** <term>Spagyrist (Latin: spagiri): A practitioner of "spagyria," the alchemical art of separating substances into their core elements and recombining them in a purified, more powerful state.</term>, one metal shall lose its own essence and become another. This is a fact that made a fool of the baseless **Aristotle** <note>The author refers to the ancient Greek philosopher whose theories dominated medieval science. Alchemists often mocked Aristotle for his theoretical approach, which they believed was proven wrong by their practical experiments.</note> in his philosophy, etc. 

The peasants in **Hungary** <note>original: "Ungern"</note>, when they lay a piece of **iron** <note>original: "eisen"</note> for a time in the **Zipser springs** <note>These mineral springs in the Spiš (German: Zips) region of modern-day Slovakia were famous in the Middle Ages. In **Kutná Hora** <note>original: "Kuttēberg" — A famous silver mining town in the Kingdom of Bohemia (modern-day Czech Republic).</note>, they pour out a **pyrite leach** <gloss>a liquor or solution derived from iron pyrites, often rich in copper vitriol</gloss>, in which iron is quickly refined into good, stable **copper**; indeed, it is even softer under the hammer than natural copper. May the divine action and power grant this to us, in whose absolute authority, might, and reign of grace all beginnings, middles, and ends reside.

## The First Book

The ancients possessed many different styles and characteristics in their manner of writing about the stone of wisdom for long life and the foundation of all arts. This stone is then rightly and justly—and not with an incorrect name—called the *philosophers' stone* <note>original: *lapis philosophorum* — a legendary substance in alchemy said to enable the transmutation of base metals into gold and to grant eternal life.</note>. However, they described this stone with such confused words and tedious procedures <note>original: *processen* — the specific technical steps or chemical operations in the alchemical "work."</note> that it would be no surprise if everyone felt a distaste for reading such writings—to say nothing of actually producing the *philosophers' stone* <note>original: *lapidem philosophorum*</note> from them. Furthermore, their procedures are also far wide of the mark.

<summary>The author concludes a prayer for divine guidance and begins "The First Book," criticizing ancient alchemical texts for being intentionally confusing and technically inaccurate regarding the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>divine power, The First Book, stone of wisdom, philosophers' stone, alchemical processes, ancient writings, critique of alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 335 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of previous authors' errors, asserting that their work was based on imagination rather than wisdom. It then transitions into the spiritual requirements for alchemy and begins a specific discussion on the relationship between Sulfur and Mercury in the creation of the "Philosopher's Gold."</meta>

...fail; for they are founded only upon their own imaginings and have not been written wisely, as we intend to proceed here.

This tincture <note>original: *tinctur* — in alchemy, a potent liquid or substance capable of transmuting metals or healing the body.</note> is—just like our gold—the most noble medicine, intended only for the person to whom it is granted through the Divine Will. Therefore, human wisdom and the senses cannot attain it without the addition of divine gifts.

## Argument Concerning the Mercury 
## of the Philosophers.
<note>original: *Argumentum de mercurio philosophorum*</note>

Sulfur <note>original: *sulphur* — one of the "Three Primes" of alchemy, representing the masculine, combustible, and active principle of nature.</note> possesses the mother to bring forth this fruit. However, sulfur is not the seed; rather, it is only through it that nature—that is, our gold—is led to the mother. Then, once she has carried this same fruit for forty weeks after conception or cohabitation <note>original: *Beischlaff* — alchemists frequently used the metaphor of human reproduction to describe the chemical union and "gestation" of substances.</note>, the fruit of mercury...

<summary>The author emphasizes that the alchemical tincture is a divine gift rather than a product of mere human intellect. He begins a metaphorical explanation of the alchemical process, describing Sulfur as the means by which "gold" is led to a "mother" to begin a forty-week gestation of Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>tincture, noble medicine, divine gift, Sulfur, Mercury of the Philosophers, alchemical gestation, philosophical gold</keywords>

--- Page 336 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 144 regarding the "Mercury of the Philosophers." The previous text described the metaphorical gestation of alchemical gold within a "mother" (Sulfur) over a period of forty weeks.</meta>

...of the philosophers is brought to light and born; but the mother must keep herself constantly warm for forty weeks, so that the fruit does not miscarry before the time of birth. ## To the Noble / Stern / Pious / Prudent / Honorable and Wise Gentlemen / the Burgomaster / and Council of the City of Basel / his gracious Lords / **Doctor Adam von Bodenstein / etc.** offers his humble, willing service and greeting.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' featuring ornate scrollwork and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

GRACIOUS / Highly-wise Gentlemen / although it is possible / that your Graces' Honorable Wisdom / may be of a healthy and strong nature from birth / and also from your parents / It is nevertheless impossible / that your great, ever-lasting business / with which Your Graces are burdened by virtue of your office / without ceasing / in fatherly care / so that good peace and the general benefit might be promoted / in right and orderly governance <note>original: *pollicey* — from the Greek 'politeia', referring to the total administration, social order, and civil regulation of a city-state.</note> / which is pleasing to God / oppressive to no one / and serves toward the great welfare <note>original: *wolfart* — the physical and social well-being of the community.</note> of the poor / [does not cause] noticeable hindrance <note>original: *hinderūg* — meaning a physical or mental strain or impediment to health caused by the stress of office.</note> to Your Honorable Wisdoms.

...of those metals [I have] explored, and dissolved the metals into their visible <term>principles</term> <note>original: *principia* — In Paracelsian chemistry, these are the 'Three Primes' (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) believed to be the fundamental components of all matter.</note>. # BOOK ONE
## ON THE POWERS OF SPIRITUAL FORCES <note>original Latin: *LIBER PRIMVS DE VIRIBVS SPIRITVALIVM*</note>.
### The first chapter.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'D' decorated with scrolled leaf motifs and floral patterns.</image-desc>
The Spirit of Life <note>original: *Spiritus vitæ* — in the medical theory of the time, this was a subtle substance or "breath" that animated the physical body</note> is a spirit that lies in all the limbs of the entire natural body, regardless of how they may be named, and in all of them it is equally twofold. One part is in spirit, the other in power, in every limb just as in the others. And it is the highest strengthening of life, from which all other limbs live. But, insofar as it distributes itself, it varies according to its location: For in the heart, the heart drives it, so <term>that</term> <note>original abbreviation: *dz* for the German *daß*</note> it also employs cardiac strength <note>original: *herzliche stercke*</note>, <term>which</term> <note>original: *dz*</note> it does not do in other limbs. Likewise in the liver, so that

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the Spirit of Life, explaining that it is a dual-natured force (spirit and power) present in every part of the body. It notes that while this spirit is universal, it takes on specific functions depending on the organ it occupies, such as the heart or liver.</summary>
<keywords>Spirit of Life, Spiritus vitae, anatomy, cardiac strength, physiology, Paracelsian medicine, vital spirits</keywords>

--- Page 351 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the liver. The catchword 'sie' (they/she/it) at the end of the previous page refers to the Spirit of Life acting upon the liver, which is the starting point here.</meta>

it also gives it great strength, which it does not otherwise do for the other limbs. The same applies to the stomach. Now, however these various strengthenings may be—whether they are observable or not—nonetheless, the single strength remains equal in all limbs. And this single strength is to be understood in this way: that the strength in the heart divides itself in relation to the strength in the **bones** <term>ossibus</term> <note>original Latin: *ossibus* — the skeletal structure</note>.

Now, it is indeed one and the same strength; for whatever maintains the **bones** <term>ossa</term> <note>Latin: *ossa* — plural of *os*, bone</note> in their power and strength must be just as empowered as that which maintains the strength in the heart; for the **bones** <term>ossibus</term> are just as important as the heart.

In the same way, the strength of the **Spirit of Life** <term>spiritus vitæ</term> <note>Latin: *spiritus vitæ* — the vital breath or force that animates the body</note> is no more in the brain than in the marrow, although it appears much stronger [in the brain]. Indeed, this is because the brain has a greater need for the **Spirit of Life** than the marrow does; yet, just as much depends on the marrow as on the brain, and it is nonetheless a single strength, etc. The same is true for the other limbs.

<summary>This page argues that the Spirit of Life is a unified force that is equally essential to all parts of the body, regardless of whether its effects are visible. It uses the comparison of the heart and brain to the bones and marrow to show that while some organs may require 'more' of this spirit to function, every part is equally dependent on it for its existence.</summary>
<keywords>Spirit of Life, Spiritus vitae, physiology, bones, marrow, brain, heart, vital force, Paracelsian medicine</keywords>
